Profunctor Optics: a Categorical Update
Bryce Clarke, Derek Elkins, Jeremy Gibbons, Fosco Loregian, Bartosz Milewski, Emily Pillmore, Mario Román

Abstract
Optics are bidirectional data accessors that capture data transformation patterns such as accessing subfields or iterating over containers. Profunctor optics are a particular choice of representation supporting modularity, meaning that we can construct accessors for complex structures by combining simpler ones. Profunctor optics have previously been studied only in an unenriched and non-mixed setting, in which both directions of access are modelled in 𝐒𝐞𝐭 \mathbf{Set} . However, functional programming languages are arguably better described by enriched categories; and we have found that some structures in the literature are actually mixed optics, with access directions modelled in different categories. Our work generalizes a classic result by Pastro and Street on Tambara theory and uses it to describe mixed V - enriched profunctor optics and to endow them with V - category structure. We provide some original families of optics and derivations, including an elementary one for traversals . Finally, we discuss a Haskell implementation. Keywords: lens, profunctor, Tambara module, coend calculus.

1
Introduction

1.1
Optics

Optics
are an abstract representation of some common patterns in
bidirectional data accessing.
The most widely known optics are
lenses
,
‘focusing’ on a subfield
A
of a larger data structure
S
through
a pair of functions
view

(S\to A)
and
update

(S\times A\to S)
that respectively retrieve and modify the field.

Lenses
have been used in functional programming as a compositional
solution to the problem of accessing fields of nested data
structures
Foster et al. 2005
(
Figure

1
).

⬇

data

Address

=

Address

{

street

::

String

,

::

,

::

String

::

{

=

}

Figure 1:
Lenses are pairs of ‘view’ and ‘update’ functions that capture
the repeating pattern of accessing subfields.
Here,
viewStreet
extracts a field from a record, and

updateStreet
updates that field.

As the understanding of these data accessors grew, different
families of optics

were introduced for a variety of different types (e.g.
prisms
for disjoint
unions and
traversals
for containers), each one of them capturing a
particular data accessing pattern (
Figure

2
).

1.2
Modularity

It is straightforward to compose two lenses, one given by
S\to A
and

S\times A\to S
and the other given by
A\to X
and
A\times X\to A
,
in order to access nested subfields. However, explicitly writing down this
composition (or explaining it to a computer) can be tedious. Intercomposability
only becomes increasingly difficult as other data accessors enter the stage:
composing a
lens
given by
S\to A
and
S\times A\to S
with a
prism
given by

A\to X+A
and
X\to A
can produce a function

S\to X\times(X\to S)+S
, which is neither a
lens
nor a
prism
but a different
optic known as
affine traversal
. Implementing explicitly a composition
like this for every possible pair of optics would be prone to errors and result
in a large codebase. However, we would like optics to behave
modularly
;
in the sense that, given two optics, it should be possible to join them into a
composite optic that directly accesses the innermost subfield.

⬇

buildString

::

Address

->

String

buildString

(
Address

s

t

c
)

=

s

++

",

"

++

t

++

",

"

++

c

verifyAddress

::

String

->

=

case

splitOn

",

"

of

[
str
,

twn
,

ctr
]

->

Right

(
Address

str

twn

ctr
)

failure

->

Left

a

asAddress

::

Prism

=

mkPrism

buildString

(

"221b

Figure 2:
A prism is given by a pair of functions
match
and

build
that account for the possiblity of failure on pattern matching.
In the figure, we verify whether a string can be parsed as an address.
The combinator
(?.)
returns the results using the
Maybe
monad (see §
6.3.1
).

Perhaps surprisingly, many implementations allow the programmer to wrap optics
into a different representation and then use
ordinary function
composition
to construct composite optics.

How is it possible to compose two constructs that are not functions using
ordinary function composition? Implementations provided by popular libraries
such as
lens

Kmett 2012–2018
,
mezzolens

O’Connor 2015b
in Haskell, or

profunctor-optics

Freeman et al. 2015–2019
in Purescript, achieve this effect by
using different representations of optics in terms of polymorphic functions and
the Yoneda lemma. This paper focuses on the encoding known as
profunctor
representation
, which is based on the isomorphism between lenses (and optics
in general) and functions that are polymorphic over profunctors with a particular
algebraic structure called a
Tambara module
. Optics under this encoding
are called
profunctor optics
.

1.3
Profunctor optics

Profunctor optics, and various other representations of lenses, were originally
proposed in functional programming as a compositional solution to the problem of
accessing fields of nested data structures
Foster et al. 2005
;
van Laarhoven 2009a
.

Different families of profunctor optics are intercomposable. When we use the profunctor
representation of optics, composing optics of different kinds becomes also a
particular case of polymorphic function composition. Together, all
families
of optics
form a powerful language for modular data access. Consider the
example of
Figure

3
, where a lens and a prism are used in
conjunction to manipulate parts of a string.

⬇

>>>

let

place

=

"221b

Baker

St,

London,

UK"

>>>

place

?.

asAddress

.

street

Just

"221b

Baker

St"

>>>

place

&

asAddress

.

street

.~

"4

Marylebone

Rd"

"4

Marylebone

Rd,

London,

UK"

Figure 3:
The composition of a prism (
asAddress
) and a lens
(
street
) produces a composite optic (
asAddress
.

street
). This optic (an “affine traversal”, see §
3.24
) is
used to parse a string and then access and modify one of its
subfields.

Moreover, optics can be used to entirely change not only the value but the type
of the focus, and propagate that change back to the original data structure.
These are called
type-variant
optics, in contrast with the

type-invariant
optics we have introduced so far (
Figure

4
).
In that case, the functions defining the optic need to account for that type
change (commonly, by also introducing polymorphism), but the internal
representation will work the same. The optics we discuss in this paper are
assumed to be type-variant, with type-invariant optics being a special case.

⬇

data

Timestamped

a

=

Timestamped

{

created
’

::

UTCTime

,

modified
’

::

UTCTime

,

contents
’

::

a

}

viewContents

::

Timestamped

a

->

a

viewContents

=

contents
’

updateContents

::

Timestamped

a

->

b

->

Timestamped

b

updateContents

x

b

=

x

{
contents
’

=

b
}

contents

::

Lens
’

a

b

(
Timestamped

a
)

(
Timestamped

b
)

contents

=

mkLens
’

viewContents

updateContents

Figure 4:
A type-variant lens that targets the contents of a value paired with
creation and modification timestamps. The lens is constructed from two functions
viewContents

and
updateContents
.

In its profunctor representation, each optic is written as a single function that
is polymorphic over profunctors with a certain algebraic structure. For
instance,
lenses
can be written as functions polymorphic over

cartesian
profunctors, whereas
prisms
can be written as functions
polymorphic over
cocartesian
profunctors
(
Pickering et al. 2017
, §3)
.
Milewski
Milewski 2017
identified these algebraic structures (cartesian
profunctors, cocartesian profunctors, …) as
Tambara
modules

Tambara 2006
and used a result by Pastro and
Street
Pastro and Street 2008
to propose a unified definition of optic. This definition
has been later extended by Boisseau and Gibbons
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
and
Riley
Riley 2018
, both using different techniques and proposing
laws for optics.

1.4
Mixed profunctor optics

However, the original result by Pastro and Street cannot be
used directly to unify all the optics that appear in practice. Our work
generalizes this result, going beyond the previous definitions of optic
to cover
mixed

(
Riley 2018
, §6.1)
and
enriched optics
.

The generalized profunctor representation theorem captures optics already
present in the literature and makes it possible to upgrade them to more
sophisticated definitions. For instance, many generalizations of
lenses

in functional programming are shown to be particular cases of a more refined
definition that uses mixed optics (
Definition

3.1
). We also show
derivations for some new optics that were not present in the literature
(
Definitions

3.31
,
3.26
and
3.8
).
Finally, Milewski
Milewski 2017
posed the problem of fitting the three basic
optics (lenses, prisms and traversals) into an elementary pattern; lenses and
prisms had been covered in his work, but traversals were missing. We present a
new description of
traversals
in terms of power series functors (
Proposition

3.22
) whose derivation
is more direct than the ones based on
traversables
as studied by
Jaskelioff and Rypacek
Jaskelioff and Rypacek 2012
.

1.5
Coend Calculus

Coend calculus
is a branch of category theory that describes the behaviour of

ends
and
coends
, certain universal objects associated with
profunctors
P:\mathbf{C}^{op}\times\mathbf{C}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
. Ends can be thought of as some form of
universal quantifier, whereas coends can be thought of as their existential counterparts.

Ends are subobjects of the product

\prod_{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)
, whereas coends result from quotienting the coproduct

\coprod_{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)
. Both take into account the fact that
P
depends on
two “terms”, covariantly on the second, and contravariantly on the first.

Definition 1.1

(Ends and coends)
.

The
end
is the equalizer of the action of morphisms on both
arguments of the profunctor, whereas the
coend
is dually defined as a coequalizer.

Ends
are usually denoted with a subscripted integral;

coends
use a superscripted integral.

\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)\coloneqq\mathrm{end}(P),\qquad\int^{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)\coloneqq\mathrm{coend}(P).

In both cases,
X
is a dummy variable, and we consider

\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)
and
\int_{Y\in\mathbf{C}}P(Y,Y)
‘equivalent modulo

\alpha
-conversion’.
The notation draws on an analogy with elementary calculus. An integral
\int f(x)\;dx

depends “covariantly” on the variable
x
defined, say, on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whereas
the differential “
dx
” can be regarded as an element of the
dual
space

(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}
. An even more striking analogy is that co/ends satisfy
a form of ‘Fubini rule’ and a ‘Dirac delta’ integration rule (see

Proposition

1.3
and
Proposition

1.2
).

Theorems involving ends and coends can be proved using their universal
properties. Here, we offer a terse account of coend calculus

Cáccamo and Winskel 2001
;
Loregian 2021
. Using the calculus based on the following rules, it
is possible to construct isomorphisms between objects of a category by means of
a chain of ‘deduction steps’.

Proposition 1.2
.

Evaluation on the identity defines the following isomorphisms, called

Yoneda
and
coYoneda
reductions, respectively.

\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathbf{C}(A,X),FX)\cong FA.\qquad\int^{X\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}(X,A)\otimes FX\cong FA,

where
\otimes
is the tensor product in the monoidal category
V

(the base of the enrichment for
\mathbf{C}
) and
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}

is a co-presheaf (there are analogous identities for presheaves).

Proposition 1.3
.

The

Fubini rule

is satisfied up to isomorphism.

\int_{X_{1}\in\mathbf{C}}\int_{X_{2}\in\mathbf{C}}P(X_{1},X_{2},X_{1},X_{2})\cong\int_{X_{2}\in\mathbf{C}}\int_{X_{1}\in\mathbf{C}}P(X_{1},X_{2},X_{1},X_{2}).

\int^{X_{1}\in\mathbf{C}}\int^{X_{2}\in\mathbf{C}}P(X_{1},X_{2},X_{1},X_{2})\cong\int^{X_{2}\in\mathbf{C}}\int^{X_{1}\in\mathbf{C}}P(X_{1},X_{2},X_{1},X_{2}).

Proposition 1.4
.

Continuity
and
cocontinuity
induce the following isomorphisms.

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(A,\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X)\right)\cong\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(A,P(X,X)).

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\int^{X\in\mathbf{C}}P(X,X),A\right)\cong\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(P(X,X),A).

Proposition 1.5
.

The set of
natural transformations
can be rewritten as a coend.

\int_{X\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{D}(FX,GX)\cong[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}](F,G)\phantom{\int}

In particular, coend calculus expressions are commonly simplified using
adjunctions

(F\dashv G)
: an adjunction is equivalently an isomorphism
\mathbf{D}(FX,Y)\cong\mathbf{C}(X,GY)
.

1.6
Contributions

Our first contribution is the derivation and partial classification of mixed
optics, covering both optics existing in the literature and some novel ones, all
following a unified definition (
Definition

2.1
). Our work completes and extends
the classification of (non-mixed) optics in
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
;
Riley 2018
.

Explicitly, we present a new family of optics in
Definition

3.8
, that
unifies new examples with some optics already present in the literature, such as

achromatic lenses

(
Boisseau 2017
, §5.2)
. We introduce an original
derivation showing that
monadic lenses

Abou-Saleh et al. 2016
are mixed
optics in
Proposition

3.6
. Similarly, in
Proposition

3.4
, we
present a novel derivation showing that the appropiate generalization of lenses
to an arbitrary monoidal category
(
Spivak 2019
, §2.2)
is not an optic but a
mixed optic. We give a unified definition of
lens
in
Definition

3.1
,
that for the first time can be specialized to all of these previous examples.
Finally, we present a new derivation of the optic known as
traversal
in

Proposition

3.22
.

Our second contribution is the definition of the enriched category of mixed profunctor
optics. The construction requires a generalization of the
Tambara modules

of
Pastro and Street 2008
that had been used to define categories of profunctor optics

Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
;
Riley 2018
to
generalized Tambara module
.
This is done in
Section

4
. As a corollary, we extend the result
that justifies the use of the profunctor representation of optics in functional
programming to the case of enriched and mixed optics (
Theorem

4.14
),
endowing them with
V
-
category structure.

1.7
Synopsis

We introduce the definition of
mixed optic
in
Section

2
.

Section

3
describes some practical examples from functional
programming and shows how they are captured by the definition.

Section

4
describes how the theory of Tambara modules can be
applied to obtain a profunctor representation for optics.
Section

5

contains concluding remarks. The Appendix (
Section

6
) introduces
the details of a full Haskell implementation.

1.8
Setting

We shall work with categories enriched over a Bénabou cosmos
({{\emph{\mathcal{V}}}},\otimes,I)
;
that is, a (small)-complete and cocomplete symmetric monoidal closed category.
In particular,
V
is enriched over itself, and we write the internal
hom-object between
A,B\in\operatorname{Obj}({\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}})
as

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(A,B)
. Our intention is to keep a close eye
on the applications in functional programming: the enriching category
V

should be thought of as the category whose objects model the types of an
idealized programming language and whose morphisms model the programs. Because
of this,
V
will be cartesian in many of the examples. We can, however,
remain agnostic as to which specific
V
we are addressing.

For calculations, we make significant use of coend calculus as described, for
instance, by Loregian
Loregian 2021
. The proofs in this paper can be carried
out without assuming choice or excluded middle, but there is an important
set-theoretical issue: in some of the examples, we compute coends over non-small
categories. We implicitly fix a suitable Grothendieck universe and our
categories are to be considered small with respect to that universe. As
Riley
(
Riley 2018
, §2)
notes, this will not be a problem in general: even if
some coends are indexed by large categories and we cannot argue their existence
using the cocompleteness of
\mathbf{Set}
, we will still find them represented by
objects in the category.

2
Optics

Our first goal is to give a unified definition that captures what it means to be
an
optic
. We have seen so far how
lenses
and
prisms
work
(
Figures

1
and
2
). A common pattern can be
extracted from these two cases.
Lenses
can be constructed when we have a
function that splits some data structure of type
S
into something of the form

M\times A
and then recombines it back to
S
. Here,
A
is the type of
the field we want to focus on, and
M
is the type combining the remaining
fields that constitute
S
. From this split, we can extract the pair of functions

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}(S\times A,S)
that define a lens.
Prisms
can be
constructed when we have a function that can split some data structure of type
S

into something of the form
M+A
and put the pieces together again.

The structure that is common to all optics is that they split a bigger data
structure of type
S\in\mathbf{C}
into some
focus
of type
A\in\mathbf{C}
and
some
context
or
residual

M\in\mathbf{M}
around it. We cannot access
the context directly, but we can still use its shape to update the original data
structure, replacing the current focus by a new one. The definition will capture
this fact imposing a quotient relation on the possible contexts; this quotient
is expressed by the dinaturality condition of a coend. The category of contexts

\mathbf{M}
will be monoidal, allowing us to compose optics with contexts
M
and

N
into an optic with context
M\otimes N
. Finally, as we want to capture

type-variant
optics, we leave open the possibility of the new focus being of a
different type
B\in\mathbf{D}
, possibly in a different category, which yields a
new data structure of type
T\in\mathbf{D}
. This is summarized in
Figure

5
.

S
T
A
B
\mathit{input}
\mathit{residual}
\mathit{focus}
\mathit{new\ focus}
\mathit{output}

Figure 5:
The common structure of an optic. We could provide semantics for diagram of this kind
in terms of profunctors, see
Román 2021
.

Multiple definitions of optics of increasing generality have been given in

Milewski 2017
;
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
;
Riley 2018
. We encompass all of them under an abstract
definition in terms of monoidal actions.

Let
(\mathbf{M},\otimes,I,a,\lambda,\rho)
be a monoidal
V
-
category

Day 1970
. Let it act on two arbitrary
V
-
categories
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}

by means of strong monoidal
V
-
functors
(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox to12.55pt{\vbox to12.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.27544pt\lower-6.27544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}
represent the
two different ways in which the context interacts with the focus: one when the
data structure is decomposed and another one, possibly different, when it is
reconstructed. By varying these two actions we will recover many examples from the
literature and introduce some new ones, as the table in
Figure

6
summarizes.

Name

Description

Actions

Base

Adapter

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\otimes\mathbf{D}(B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{id},\mathrm{id}})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Lens

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(S\bullet B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\times,\bullet})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Monoidal lens

\mathbf{CCom}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{U}}S\otimes B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\otimes,_{\mathcal{U}}\times})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Algebraic lens

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(\Psi S\bullet B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{{}_{\mathcal{U}}\times,_{\mathcal{U}}\bullet})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Monadic lens

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(S,A)\times{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(S\times B,\Psi T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\times,\rtimes})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Linear lens

\mathbf{C}(S,[B,T]\bullet A)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\bullet,\otimes})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Prism

\mathbf{C}(S,T\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\bullet,+})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Coalg. prism

\mathbf{C}(S,\Theta T\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{{}_{\mathcal{U}}\bullet,_{\mathcal{U}}+})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Grate

\mathbf{D}([S,A]\bullet B,T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\{\ ,\ \},\bullet})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Glass

\mathbf{C}(S\times[[S,A],B],T)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\times[\ ,\ ],\times[\ ,\ ]})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Affine traversal

\mathbf{C}(S,T+A\otimes\{B,T\})

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{+\otimes,+\otimes})

{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}},\times

Traversal

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,\sum\nolimits^{n}A^{n}\otimes[B^{n},T])

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{Pw},\mathrm{Pw}})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Kaleidoscope

\sum\nolimits_{n}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}([A^{n},B],[S^{n},T])

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{App},\mathrm{App}})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Setter

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}([A,B],[S,T])

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{ev}})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Fold

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,\mathcal{L}A)

(\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{Foldable},\ast})

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},\otimes

Figure 6:
Table of optics, together with their explicit description and their
generating monoidal actions. The bullet represents some monoidal action; the brackets represent some monoidal action from the opposite category.

The purpose of an abstract unified definition is twofold: firstly, it provides a
framework to classify existing optics and explore new ones, as we do in

Section

3
; and secondly, it enables a unified profunctor
representation, which we present in
Section

4
.

3
Examples of optics

3.1
Lenses and prisms

Lenses
can be seen as accessors for a particular subfield of a data
structure. In its basic form, they are given by a pair of functions: the

view
function that accesses the subfield; and
the
update
function that overwrites its
contents.

The basic definition of
lens

Oles 1982
;
Foster et al. 2005
;
Palmer 2007
has been generalized in many different directions.
Monadic lenses

Abou-Saleh et al. 2016
,

lenses in a symmetric monoidal category

(
Spivak 2019
, §2.2)
,

linear lenses

(
Riley 2018
, §4.8)
or
achromatic lenses

(
Boisseau 2017
, §5.2)
are some of them. These generalizations were not meant to be
mutually compatible. They use the monoidal structure in different
ways and introduce monadic effects in different parts of the signature. Some
were not presented as
optics
, and a profunctor representation for them
was not considered. We present a unified description that captures all of these
variants.

We present two derivations of lenses as mixed optics that capture all of the
variants mentioned before, together with new ones, and endow them with a unified profunctor
representation (
Theorem

4.14
).

The first derivation is based on cartesian structure. It generalizes the original
derivation
Milewski 2017
and captures
lenses in a symmetric monoidal
category
(
Definition

3.3
) and
monadic lenses

(
Definition

3.5
). It is then refined to cover
achromatic
lenses
and describe some new variants of optics missing in the literature. The
second derivation slightly generalizes
linear lenses

(
Riley 2018
, §4.8)
and uses the closed structure instead.

3.1.1
Lenses

Most variants of lenses rely on a cartesian monoidal structure. Throughout this
section, we take a cartesian closed category
({{\emph{\mathcal{W}}}},\times,1)
as our
base for enrichment. During the rest of the paper we will explicitly use
W
to
refer to a cartesian monoidal base of enrichment and use
V
to refer to a
not-necessarily-cartesian base of enrichment. A monoidal
W
-
category

(\mathbf{C},\times,1)
is said to be
cartesian
if there exist
W
-
natural isomorphisms

\mathbf{C}(Z,X\times Y)\cong\mathbf{C}(Z,X)\times\mathbf{C}(Z,Y)
and
\mathbf{C}(X,1)\cong 1
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a cartesian
W
-
category with a monoidal
W
-
action

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
to an arbitrary
W
-
category
\mathbf{D}
.
A
lens
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Lens}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(S\bullet B,T).

Proposition 3.2
.

Lenses
are mixed optics (as in
Definition

2.1
) for the actions of
the cartesian product
(\times)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
and

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
. That is,

\hyperlink{linklens}{\mathbf{Lens}}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(\times,\bullet)}
.

Proof.

The universal property of the product can be summarized as it being right
adjoint to the diagonal functor
(\Delta)\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{2}
.

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}(S,C\times A)\times\mathbf{D}(C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

\Delta\dashv(\times)
}

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}(S,C)\times\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(S\bullet B,T).\qed

This definition can be specialized to the pair

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}(S\times B,T)
if we take
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{D}
and we let

(\bullet)
be the cartesian product. It is, however, more general than that, and it
captures the following examples (
Definitions

3.3
and
3.5
).

3.1.2
Lenses in a symmetric monoidal category

Definition 3.3
.

A
monoidal lens

(
Spivak 2019
, §2.2)
in a symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{C}
is a
view and update
pair where
the view is a commutative comonoid homomorphism,

{{\emph{\mathbf{MonLens}_{\otimes}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{CCom}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{U}}S\otimes B,T).

Here
{\mathcal{U}}
represents the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathbf{CCom}\to\mathbf{C}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Monoidal lenses in a symmetric monoidal category are a particular case of
Definition

3.1
.

Proof.

The category of cocommutative comonoids
\mathbf{CCom}
over a category
\mathbf{C}
can
be given a cartesian structure in such a way that the forgetful functor

{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathbf{CCom}\to\mathbf{C}
is strict monoidal (Fox shows a
stronger result
Fox 1976
). By Proposition
3.2
, we can show

\hyperlink{linkmlens}{\mathbf{MonLens}}_{\otimes}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(\otimes,\bullet)}
where

(\bullet)
is given by
S\bullet A\coloneqq{\mathcal{U}}S\otimes A
.
∎

3.1.3
Monadic lenses

Definition 3.5
.

Monadic lenses
(
Abou-Saleh et al. 2016
, §2.3)
allow for monadic effects in the

update
function (an example is Figure
7
). For

\Psi\colon{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}
a
W
-
monad,

{{\emph{\mathbf{MndLens}_{\Psi}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(S,A)\times{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(S\times B,\Psi T).

Proposition 3.6
.

Monadic lenses are a particular case of
Definition

3.1
.

Proof.

Every
W
-
endofunctor is
strong
: this is because a functor
F\colon{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}
with a strength is the same thing as a
W
-enrichment of the functor
Levy 2019
. Thus, the

W
-
monad
\Psi
comes with a
W
-
natural family

\theta_{X,Y}\colon X\times\Psi(Y)\to\Psi(X\times Y)
. This induces a

W
-
action

(\rtimes)\colon{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}\times\operatorname{Kl}(\Psi)\to\operatorname{Kl}(\Psi)
,
with
\operatorname{Kl}(\Psi)
the Kleisli category of the monad; defined, on morphisms, as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(X,Y)\times{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(A,\Psi B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(X\times A,Y\times\Psi B)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(X\times A,\Psi(Y\times B)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\times)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Using that

\operatorname{Kl}_{\Psi}(S\rtimes B,T)\coloneqq{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}(S\times B,\Psi T)
,
monadic lenses are lenses (as in
Definition

3.1
),
\hyperlink{linkmonadiclens}{\mathbf{MndLens}}_{\Psi}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(\times,\rtimes)}
,
where
(\bullet)
is given by

(\rtimes)\colon{\hyperlink{linkwenrichment}{\mathcal{W}}}\times\operatorname{Kl}(\Psi)\to\operatorname{Kl}(\Psi)
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

This technique is similar to the one used by
(
Riley 2018
, §4.9)
to describe a non-mixed
variant called
effectful lenses
.

3.1.4
Algebraic lenses

We can further generalize
Definition

3.1
if we allow the

context
over which we take the coend to be an algebra for a fixed
monad. The motivation is that lenses with a context like this appear to have
direct applications in programming; for instance, the
achromatic
lenses
of
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
are a particular case of this definition. These

algebraic lenses
should not be confused with the previous

monadic lenses
in
Definition

3.5
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\Psi\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
be a
W
-
monad on a cartesian
W
-
category

\mathbf{C}
. Let
(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
be a monoidal
W
-
action
on an arbitrary
W
-
category
\mathbf{D}
. An
algebraic lens
is an element
of

{{\emph{\mathbf{AlgLens}}}}_{\Psi}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(\Psi S\bullet B,T).

Proposition 3.9
.

Algebraic lenses are mixed optics for the
actions of the product by the carrier of an algebra

(_{\mathcal{U}}\times)\colon\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
and

(_{\mathcal{U}}\bullet)\colon\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
. That is,

\hyperlink{linkalgebraiclens}{\mathbf{AlgLens}}_{\Psi}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(_{\mathcal{U}}\times,_{\mathcal{U}}\bullet)}
.

Proof.

The
W
-
category of algebras is
cartesian, making the forgetful functor
{\mathcal{U}}\colon\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}\to\mathbf{C}
monoidal;

{\mathcal{U}}(\bullet)\times\bullet
defines a monoidal action. The forgetful
{\mathcal{U}}
has a left adjoint

\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}
such that
{\mathcal{U}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}=\Psi
.

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}}}\mathbf{C}(S,{\mathcal{U}}C\times A)\times\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{U}}C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(\times)\dashv\Delta
}

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}}}\mathbf{C}(S,{\mathcal{U}}C)\times\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{U}}C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}\dashv{\mathcal{U}}
}

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}}}\operatorname{EM}_{\Psi}(\mathcal{F}^{\Psi}S,C)\times\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{U}}C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{D}(\Psi S\bullet B,T).\qed

Remark 3.10
.

Algebraic lenses are a new optic. When
(\bullet)
is the cartesian product,
algebraic lenses are given by the usual
view
function that accesses
the subfield, and a variant of the
update
function that takes
the original source as a computation rather than a value.

Example 3.11
.

The algebraic lens for the list monad
\mathcal{L}\colon{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
is a
new kind of optic that we dub a
classifying lens
. This is given by two
functions, the usual
view
function that accesses the focus, and a

classify
function that takes a list of examples together with some
piece of data and produces a new example.
A classifying lens can be trained with a dataset
(Figure
8
) to classify a new focus into a complete data
structure. A learning algorithm (in this case, a naive version of

nearest neighbor
) defines an algebraic lens that can be used to
classify foci into full data structures.

).

Remark 3.12
.

The algebraic lens for the
maybe monad

\mathcal{M}\colon{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
was studied in
(
Boisseau 2017
, §5.2)
under the name
achromatic lens
. It is motivated by
the fact that, sometimes in practice, lenses come naturally equipped with a

create
function
(
Foster et al. 2005
, §3)
along the usual
view

and
update
.
For this implementation, we note that

\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{M}S\times B,T)\cong\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}(S\times B,T)\times\mathbf{C}(B,T).

Remark 3.13
.

The name
achromatic lens
can also refer to a different proposal in
(
Riley 2018
, §4.10)
,

\mathbf{C}(S,[B,T]+1)\times\mathbf{C}(S,A)\times\mathbf{C}(B,T).

This is the optic for the action
\odot\colon\mathbf{C}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
defined by

M\odot A\coloneqq(M+1)\times A
. It is not exactly equivalent to the

achromatic lens
in
Boisseau 2017
, which is the optic for the action

(\times)\colon\textrm{$\mathcal{M}$-Alg}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
defined by

{\mathcal{U}}M\times A
. It can be implemented by
view
and
create

functions, this time together with a
maybeUpdate
that is allowed to
fail.

3.1.5
Lenses in a closed category

Linear lenses are a different generalization of lenses which relies on a closed
monoidal structure. Their advantage is that we do not need to require our
enriching category to be cartesian anymore.

Definition 3.14

(
(
Riley 2018
, §4.8)
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{D},\otimes,[\ ,\ ])
be a right closed
V
-
category with a monoidal

V
-
action
(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{D}\otimes\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
to an arbitrary

V
-
category
\mathbf{C}
. A
linear lens
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{LinearLens}}}}_{\otimes,\bullet}((A,B),(S,T))\cong\mathbf{C}(S,[B,T]\bullet A).

Proposition 3.15
.

Linear lenses are mixed optics (as in
Definition

2.1
) for the
actions of the monoidal product
(\otimes)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
and

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
. That is,

\hyperlink{linklinearlens}{\mathbf{LinearLens}}_{\otimes,\bullet}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\bullet,\otimes}

Proof.

The monoidal product has a right adjoint given by the exponential.

\displaystyle\int^{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{C}(S,D\bullet A)\otimes\mathbf{D}(D\otimes B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(-\otimes B)\dashv[B,-]
}

\displaystyle\int^{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{C}(S,D\bullet A)\otimes\mathbf{D}(D,[B,T])

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(S,[B,T]\bullet A).\qed

3.1.6
Prisms

Prisms pattern-match on data structures and handle a possible failure to
match. They are given by a
match
function that tries to access the
matched structure and a
build
function that constructs an abstract type
from one of its possible matchings.
Prisms happen to be lenses in the opposite category. However, they can also be
described as optics in the original category for a different pair of actions. We
will provide a derivation of prisms that is dual to our derivation of lenses for
a cartesian structure. This derivation specializes to the pair

\mathbf{C}(S,T+A)\times\mathbf{C}(B,T)
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a cocartesian
W
-
category with a monoidal
W
-
action

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
to an arbitrary category
\mathbf{C}
. A

prism
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Prism}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,T\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T).

In other words, a prism from
(S,T)
to
(A,B)
is a lens from
(T,S)

to
(B,A)
in the opposite categories
\mathbf{D}^{op}
and
\mathbf{C}^{op}
. However,
they can also be seen as optics from
(A,B)
to
(S,T)
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Prisms are mixed optics (as in
Definition

2.1
) for the actions of
the coproduct
(+)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
and

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
. That is,

\hyperlink{linkprism}{\mathbf{Prism}}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(\bullet,+)}
.

Proof.

The coproduct
(+)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
is left adjoint to the diagonal
functor.

\displaystyle\int^{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{C}(S,D\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(D+B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(+)\dashv\Delta
}

\displaystyle\int^{D\in\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{C}(S,D\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(D,T)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(S,T\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T).\qed

Remark 3.18

(Prisms in a symmetric monoidal category)
.

A prism in a symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{C}
is a match and build pair where
the build is a monoid homomorphism,

\mathbf{MonPrism}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,{\mathcal{U}}T\otimes A)\times\mathbf{CMon}(B,T).

Remark 3.19

(Prisms with coalgebraic context)
.

Let
\Theta
be a
W
-
comonad in a cocartesian
W
-
category
\mathbf{D}
and

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{D}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
a monoidal
W
-
action. A
coalgebraic prism
is an element of

\mathbf{AlgPrism}_{\Theta}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,\Theta T\bullet A)\times\mathbf{D}(B,T).

The coalgebraic
variant is given by a
cMatch
function, that captures the failure into
a comonad, and the usual
build
function.

3.2
Traversals

Traversals extract the elements of a finitary container
O’Connor 2015b
into an ordered list, allowing us
to iterate over the elements without altering the container
(Figure
9
). Traversals are constructed from a single

extract :: s -> ([a], [b] -> t)
function that both outputs the elements
and takes a new list to update them. Usually, we require the length of the input to
the function of type
[b] -> t
to be the same as the length of
[a]
.
This restriction can be also encoded into the type when dependent types are
available.

⬇

let

places

=

[

"43

Adlington

Wilmslow,

United

Kingdom"

,

"26

Westcott

Rd,

Princeton,

USA"

,

"St

James’s

Square,

London,

United

Kingdom"

]

::

Traversal

a

[
a
]

each

=

(\
x

->

(
x

,

id
))

,

"ST

Kingdom"

The composition of a traversal (
each
) with a prism
(

) and a lens (
street
) is used to parse a collection of
strings and modify one of the resulting subfields.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a symmetric monoidal closed
V
-
category with countably
infinite coproducts.
A
traversal
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Traversal}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes[B^{n},T]\right).

Remark 3.21
.

Let
(\mathbb{N},+)
be the free strict monoidal
V
-
category on one object.
Ends and coends indexed by
\mathbb{N}
coincide with products and
coproducts, respectively. Here
A^{(-)}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{C}
is the
unique monoidal
V
-
functor sending the generator of
\mathbb{N}
to

A\in\mathbf{C}
. Each functor
X\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{C}
induces a

power
series

{{\emph{\operatorname{Pw}}}}_{X}(A)=\int\nolimits^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes X_{n}.

This defines an action

\hyperlink{linkpw}{\operatorname{Pw}}\colon[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
sending the indexed
family to its power series (see Remark
3.23
). We propose a
derivation of the
traversal
as the optic for power series.

Proposition 3.22
.

Traversals are optics (as in
Definition

2.1
) for power series.
That is,
\hyperlink{linktraversal}{\mathbf{Traversal}}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\hyperlink{linkpw}{\operatorname{Pw}},\hyperlink{linkpw}{\operatorname{Pw}}}
.

Proof.

The derivation generalizes that of linear lenses (
Definition

3.14
).

\displaystyle\int^{X\in[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]}\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes X_{n}\right)\otimes\mathbf{C}\left(\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}B^{n}\otimes X_{n},T\right)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Continuity
}

\displaystyle\int^{X\in[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]}\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes X_{n}\right)\otimes\int_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{C}\left(X_{n}\otimes B^{n},T\right)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(-\otimes B^{n})\dashv[B^{n},-]
}

\displaystyle\int^{X\in[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]}\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes X_{n}\right)\otimes\int_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{C}\left(X_{n},[B^{n},T]\right)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Natural transformation
}

\displaystyle\int^{X\in[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]}\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes X_{n}\right)\otimes[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]\left(X_{(-)},[B^{(-)},T]\right)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes[B^{n},T]\right).\qed

The derivation from the general definition of optic to the concrete description
of lenses and prisms in functional programming was first described by

Pickering et al. 2017
and
Milewski 2017
, but finding a derivation of
traversals like the one presented here, fitting the same elementary pattern as
lenses or prisms, was left as an open problem. It should be noted, however, that
derivations of the traversal as the optic for a certain kind of functor called

Traversables
(which should not be confused with traversals themselves)
have been previously described by
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
;
Riley 2018
. For a derivation
using Yoneda,
Riley 2018
recalls a parameterised adjunction that has an
equational proof in the work of
Jaskelioff and O’Connor 2015
. These two derivations do
not contradict each other: two different classes of functors can generate the
same optic; if, for instance, the adjunction describing both of them gives rise
to the same monad. This seems to be the case here: traversables are coalgebras
for a comonad and power series are the cofree coalgebras for the same
comonad
(
Román 2019
, §4.2)
.

In the
\mathbf{Sets}\mbox{-based}
case, the relation between traversable functors, applicative functors
McBride and Paterson 2008
and these power series functors has been studied by
Jaskelioff and Rypacek 2012
.

Remark 3.23
.

The
V
-
functor
\hyperlink{linkpw}{\operatorname{Pw}}\colon[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
is
actually a monoidal action thanks to the fact that two power series
functors compose into a power series functor.

\displaystyle\int^{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\int^{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{n}\otimes C_{n}\right)^{m}\otimes D_{m}

\displaystyle\cong

{Product distributes over colimits}

\displaystyle\int^{m}\int^{n_{1},\dots,n_{m}}A^{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes A^{n_{m}}\otimes C_{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes C_{n_{m}}\otimes D_{m}

\displaystyle\cong

{Rearranging terms}

\displaystyle\int^{k\in\mathbb{N}}A^{k}\otimes\left(\sum_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{m}=k}C_{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes C_{n_{m}}\otimes D_{m}\right).

We are then considering an implicit non-symmetric monoidal structure where the
monoidal product

(\bullet)\colon[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]\otimes[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]\to[\mathbb{N},\mathbf{C}]

of
C_{n}
and
D_{n}
can be written as follows; and the relevant copowers exist because
of
\mathbf{C}
having an initial object.

\int^{m}\int^{n_{1},\dots,n_{m}}\mathbb{N}(n_{1}+\dots+n_{m},k)\cdot C_{n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes C_{n_{m}}\otimes D_{m}.

This is precisely the structure described by Kelly
(
Kelly 2005b
, §8)
for the
study of non-symmetric operads. A similar monoidal structure is described there
when we substitute
\mathbb{N}
by
(\mathbb{P},+)
, the

V
-
category of permutations
defined as the free strict symmetric
monoidal category on one object. The same derivation can be repeated with this
new structure to obtain an optic similar to the traversal, with the difference that
elements are not ordered explicitly, and given by

\mathbf{C}\left(S,\int^{n\in\mathbb{P}}A^{n}\otimes[B^{n},T]\right).

3.2.1
Affine traversals

Affine traversals
strictly generalize prisms and linear lenses in the
non-mixed case allowing a
lens-like
accessing pattern to fail; they are
used to give a concrete representation of the composition between a lens and a
prism. An affine traversal is implemented by a single
access
function.

Definition 3.24
.

Let
W
be cartesian closed and let
\mathbf{C}
be a symmetric monoidal closed

W
-
category that is also cocartesian. An
affine traversal
is an
element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Affine}}}}_{\otimes}\left((A,B),(S,T)\right):=\mathbf{C}(S,T+A\otimes[B,T]).

Proposition 3.25
.

Affine traversals are optics (as in
Definition

3.1
) for the action

(+\otimes)\colon\mathbf{C}^{2}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
that sends
C,D\in\mathbf{C}
to the
functor
C+D\otimes(-)
. That is,

\hyperlink{linkaffine}{\mathbf{Affine}}_{\otimes}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{(+\otimes),(+\otimes)}
.

Proof.

The action uses that the monoidal product, which is in this case a left
adjoint, distributes over the coproduct.

\displaystyle\int^{C,D}\mathbf{C}(S,C+D\otimes A)\times\mathbf{C}(C+D\otimes B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(+)\dashv\Delta
}

\displaystyle\int^{C,D}\mathbf{C}(S,C+D\otimes A)\times\mathbf{C}(C,T)\times\mathbf{C}(D\otimes B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

{
Adjunction

(-\otimes B)\dashv[B,-]
}

\displaystyle\int^{D}\mathbf{C}(S,T+D\otimes A)\times\mathbf{C}(D,[B,T])

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(S,T+A\otimes[B,T]).\qed

3.2.2
Kaleidoscopes

Applicative
functors are commonly used in functional programming as
they provide a convenient generalization to monads with better properties for
composition; they form the
V
-
category
{{\emph{\mathbf{App}}}}
of monoids with
respect to Day convolution
(\ast)\colon[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\times[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
,
which is defined as

(F\ast G)(A)=\int^{X,Y\in{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(X\otimes Y,A)\otimes FX\otimes GY.

Alternatively, they are lax monoidal
V
-
functors for the cartesian structure.
It is natural to ask what is the optic associated with applicative functors. We know
from a basic result in category theory
(
Mac Lane 1978
, §VII, Theorem 2)
that,
as the category
[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
has coproducts indexed by the natural numbers, and Day convolution
distributes over them, the free applicative functor can be computed as the
colimit
(I+F+F^{\ast 2}+F^{\ast 3}+\dots)
. Having characterized free
applicative functors, computing their associated optic amounts to an application
of coend calculus.

Definition 3.26
.

A
kaleidoscope
is an element of

\mathbf{Kaleidoscope}\left((A,B),(S,T)\right):=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left([A^{n},B],[S^{n},T]\right).

Proposition 3.27
.

Kaleidoscopes are optics for the action of applicative functors.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}\colon\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
be the forgetful functor from the category
of applicatives.

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,{\mathcal{U}}FA)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Yoneda
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(S,\int_{C\in{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}}\big[[A,C],{\mathcal{U}}FC\big]\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Continuity
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}\int_{C}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(S,\big[[A,C],{\mathcal{U}}FC\big]\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

([A,C]\otimes-)\dashv\big[[A,C],-\big]
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}\left(\int_{C}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left([A,C]\otimes S,{\mathcal{U}}FC\right)\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Natural transformation
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\left([A,-]\otimes S,{\mathcal{U}}F\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{Adjunction of free applicatives}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}}\hyperlink{linkapp}{\mathbf{App}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[A^{n},-]\otimes S^{n},F\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S^{n}\otimes[A^{n},B],T\right)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Continuity
}

{
Adjunction

(S^{n}\otimes-)\dashv[S^{n},-]
}

\displaystyle\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left([A^{n},B],[S^{n},T]\right).\qed

Remark 3.28
.

The free applicative we construct here is known in Haskell programming as the

FunList
applicative
van Laarhoven 2009b
.
In the same way that traversables are written in
terms of lists, we can approximate Kaleidoscopes as a single function

aggregate :: ([a] -> b) -> ([s] -> t)
that takes a folding for the foci and outputs a folding for
the complete data structure. Kaleidoscopes are a new optic, and we propose to
use them as accessors for pointwise foldable data structures.

Kaleidoscopes pose a problem on the side of applications: they cannot be
composed with lenses to produce new kaleidoscopes. This is because
the constraint defining them (
Applicative
) is not a
superclass of the constraint defining lenses: a functor given by a product is
not applicative, in general. However, a functor given by a product
by a
monoid
is applicative. This means that applicatives can be composed with
lenses whose residual is a monoid, which are precisely the newly defined

classifying lenses
(
Definition

3.8
).

⬇

representative

::

Kaleidoscope

Float

Measurements

representative

=

mkKaleidoscope

aggregate

where

aggregate

f

l

=

Measurements

(
f

(
fmap

sepalLe

l
))

(
f

(
fmap

sepalWi

l
))

(
f

(
fmap

petalLe

l
))

(
f

(
fmap

petalWi

l
))

iris

&

measure
.
representative

>-

mean

>>>

Iris

Versicolor
;

Sepal

(5.843,

3.054);

Petal

(3.758,

1.198)

Figure 10:
Following the previous Example
8
, a kaleidoscope (
representative
) is composed with an algebraic lens to create a new point in the dataset by aggregating measurements with some function (
mean
,
maximum
) and then classifying it.

3.3
Grates

Grates
create a new structure when provided with a way of creating a new focus from
a view function. They are given by a single
grate
function with the form
of a nested continuation.

Definition 3.29
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a symmetric closed
V
-
category (as in
Day and Laplaza 1978
). Let

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
be an arbitrary action. A

grate
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Grate}}}}\left((A,B),(S,T)\right):=\mathbf{D}([S,A]\bullet B,T).

Proposition 3.30
.

Grates are mixed optics for the action of the exponential and

(\bullet)\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
. That is,

\hyperlink{linkgrate}{\mathbf{Grate}}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{[\ ,\ ],\bullet}
.

Proof.

We know from
(
Day and Laplaza 1978
, Proposition 1.2)
that the adjunction

[-,A]^{op}\dashv[-,A]
holds in any symmetric monoidal category.

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}(S,[C,A])\otimes\mathbf{D}(C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

[-,A]^{op}\dashv[-,A]
}

\displaystyle\int^{C\in\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}(C,[S,A])\otimes\mathbf{D}(C\bullet B,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}([S,A]\bullet B,T)\qed

The description of a grate and its profunctor representation in terms of
“closed” profunctors was first reported by Deikun and
O’Connor
O’Connor 2015a
; it can be seen as a consequence of the profunctor
representation theorem (
Theorem

4.14
).

3.3.1
Glasses

Glasses
are a new optic that strictly generalizes grates and lenses for
the cartesian case. In functional programming, glasses can be implemented by a
single function
glass
that takes a way of transforming
views
into
new foci and uses it to update the data structure. We propose to use them as a
concrete representation of the composition of lenses and grates.

Definition 3.31
.

A
glass
in a cartesian closed
W
-
category is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Glass}}}}\left((A,B),(S,T)\right):=\mathbf{C}(S\times[[S,A],B],T).

Proposition 3.32
.

Glasses are optics for the action

(\bullet\times[\bullet,-])\colon\mathbf{C}^{op}\times\mathbf{C}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
that sends
C,D\in\mathbf{C}

to the
W
-
functor
D\times[C,-]
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\int^{C,D}\mathbf{C}(S,C\times[D,A])\times\mathbf{C}([D,B]\times C,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

\Delta\dashv(\times)
}

\displaystyle\int^{C,D}\mathbf{C}(S,C)\times\mathbf{C}(S,[D,A])\times\mathbf{C}([D,B]\times C,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\int^{D}\mathbf{C}(S,[D,A])\times\mathbf{C}([D,B]\times S,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

[-,A]^{op}\dashv[-,A]
}

\displaystyle\int^{D}\mathbf{C}(D,[S,A])\times\mathbf{C}([D,B]\times S,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}([[S,A],B]\times S,T).\qed

3.4
Getters, reviews and folds

Some constructions, such as plain morphisms, can be regarded as degenerate
cases of optics. We will describe these constructions (
getters
,

reviews
and
folds

Kmett 2012–2018
) as mixed optics. All of them set
one of the two base categories to be the terminal category and, contrary to most
optics, they act only unidirectionally. We will derive the usual
implementations of getters as
s -> a
, of reviews as
b -> t
, and
of folds as
s -> [a]
. Their profunctor representation can be seen as a
covariant or contravariant application of the Yoneda lemma.

Definition 3.33
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an arbitrary
V
-
category. Getters (morphisms
\mathbf{C}(S,A)
) are
degenerate optics for the trivial action on the covariant side. Reviews (morphisms

\mathbf{C}(B,T)
) are degenerate optics for the trivial action on the contravariant
side. In other words, we can obtain plain morphisms as a particular case of optic when

\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{1}
or
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{1}
,
respectively.

The category of
foldable
functors,
\mathbf{Foldable}
, is the slice category on the list
functor
{{\emph{\mathcal{L}}}}\colon{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
, also known as the free monoid functor.
Using the fact that
L
is a monad, the slice category

[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]/\hyperlink{linklist}{\mathcal{L}}
can be made monoidal in such a way that the forgetful functor

[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]/\hyperlink{linklist}{\mathcal{L}}\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
becomes monoidal.

Definition 3.34
.

Folds are optics for the action of foldable functors and the trivial
action on the contravariant side.

\mathbf{Fold}((A,\ast),(S,\ast))=\int^{F\in\mathbf{Foldable}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,FA)\cong{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,\hyperlink{linklist}{\mathcal{L}}A).

Folds admit a concrete description,
{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,\hyperlink{linklist}{\mathcal{L}}A)
, which can be reached from
the definition of coends as colimits. A coend from a diagram category with a
terminal object is determined by the value at the terminal object and here,
L
is the terminal object. The same
technique can be used to prove that the optic for the slice category over a
monad
\mathcal{G}\colon{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
has a concrete form given by

\mathbf{C}(S,\mathcal{G}A)
.

3.5
Setters and adapters

We finish our examples of optics with two extremes: the actions from the initial and terminal actions. These are the identity action
\mathrm{id}\colon[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
and picking the monoidal unit,
\mathrm{I}\colon\mathbf{1}\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
.

Definition 3.35
.

A setter
Kmett 2012–2018
is an element of

{{\emph{\mathbf{Setter}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}([A,B],[S,T]).

Proposition 3.36
.

Setters are optics for identity action

\mathrm{id}\colon[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
. That is,

\hyperlink{linksetter}{\mathbf{Setter}}\cong\hyperlink{linkoptic}{\mathbf{Optic}}_{\mathrm{id},\mathrm{id}}
.

Proof.

The concrete derivation is described by Riley
(
Riley 2018
, §4.5.2)
. There, it is required that functors are strong; this requirement holds automatically for every enriched functor in the base of enrichment
Levy 2019
.

\displaystyle\int^{F\in[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,FA)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Yoneda
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]}\left(\int_{C\in{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}}[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(A,C),{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,FC)]\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{Copower}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]}\left(\int_{C\in{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S\otimes[A,C],FC)\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Natural transformation
}

\displaystyle\int^{F\in[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]}[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}](S\otimes[A,-],F)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(FB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S\otimes[A,B],T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

(S\otimes-)\dashv[S,-]
}

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}([A,B],[S,T]).\qed

Remark 3.37
.

In functional programming, we implicitly restrict to the case where
V
is
a cartesian category, and we curry this description to obtain the usual
representation of setters as a single
over
function.

Definition 3.38
.

Adapters
Kmett 2012–2018
are morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{op}\otimes\mathbf{D}
. They
are optics for the action
\mathrm{Id}\colon\mathbf{1}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
.

{{\emph{\mathbf{Adapter}}}}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\mathbf{C}(S,A)\otimes\mathbf{D}(B,T).

3.6
Optics for (co)free

A common pattern that appears across many optic derivations is that of computing the optic associated with a class of functors using an adjunction to allow for an application of the Yoneda lemma. This observation can be generalized to a class of concrete optics.

Consider some
V
-
endofunctor
H\colon{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
. Any objects
A,B,S,T\in{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
can be regarded as functors from the unit
V
-
category. The following isomorphisms are the consequence of the fact that left and right global Kan extensions are left and right adjoints to precomposition, respectively.

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,HA)\cong[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}](\mathsf{Lan}_{A}S,H),

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(HB,T)\cong[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}](H,\mathsf{Ran}_{B}T).

These extensions exist in
V
and they are given by the formulas

\mathsf{Lan}_{A}S\cong[A,-]\otimes S,\quad\mathsf{Ran}_{B}T\cong\big[[-,B],T\big].

Proposition 3.39

(
(
Román 2019
, §3.4.7)
)
.

Let the monoidal
V
-
action
{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathbf{M}\to[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
have a left adjoint
\mathcal{L}\colon[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\to\mathbf{M}
, or, dually, let it have a right adjoint
\mathcal{R}\colon[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\to\mathbf{M}
. In both of these cases the optic determined by that monoidal action has a concrete form, given by

{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathcal{UL}([A,-]\otimes S)(B),T)\quad\mbox{ or }\quad{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,\mathcal{UR}\big[[-,B],T\big](A)),

respectively.

Proof.

We prove the first case. The second one is dual.

\displaystyle\int^{M\in\mathbf{M}}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(S,{\mathcal{U}}MA)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}MB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{Kan extension}

\displaystyle\int^{M\in\mathbf{M}}[{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]\left(\mathsf{Lan}_{A}S,{\mathcal{U}}M\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}MB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Adjunction

\mathcal{L}\dashv\mathcal{U}
}

\displaystyle\int^{M\in\mathbf{M}}\mathbf{M}\left(\mathcal{L}\mathsf{Lan}_{A}S,M\right)\otimes{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{U}}MB,T)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Coyoneda
}

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}\left(\mathcal{UL}\mathsf{Lan}_{A}S(B),T\right).

4
Tambara theory

A fundamental feature of optics is that they can be represented as a single
polymorphic function. Optics in this form are called
profunctor optics

and we say this function is their
profunctor representation
. Profunctor
optics can be easily composed, even if they are from different families:
composition of optics as polymorphic functions becomes ordinary function
composition. Figure
3
shows an example of this phenomenon.
Profunctor optics are functions polymorphic over profunctors endowed with some
extra algebraic structure. This extra structure depends on the family of the
optic they represent. For instance,
lenses
are represented by functions
polymorphic over
cartesian
profunctors, while
prisms
are
represented by functions polymorphic over
cocartesian

profunctors
(
Pickering et al. 2017
, §3)
. Milewski
Milewski 2017
notes that the algebraic
structures accompanying these profunctors are precisely
Tambara
modules
, a particular kind of profunctor that has been used
to characterize the monoidal centre of convolution monoidal
categories
Tambara 2006
. Because of this correspondence, categories of
lenses or prisms can be obtained as particular cases of the “Doubles for
monoidal categories” construction defined by Pastro and Street
(
Pastro and Street 2008
, §6)
.
The
double
1
1

1

Double, as used by Pastro and Street
Pastro and Street 2008
,
should not be confused with
double
in the sense of
double
category
.
of an arbitrary monoidal
V
-
category
(\mathcal{A},\otimes,I)
, is a
promonoidal

2
2

2

A promonoidal category
\mathcal{A}
is a generalization of a monoidal category with functors replaced by profunctors. For instance, a tensor product is a profunctor
P\colon\mathcal{A}^{op}\otimes\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}
and the unit is
J\colon\mathcal{A}^{op}\to{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}

V
-
category
\mathcal{DA}
whose hom-objects are defined as

\mathcal{DA}((A,B),(S,T))\coloneqq\int^{C\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(S,C\otimes A)\otimes\mathcal{A}(C\otimes B,T).

In the particular case where
\mathcal{A}
is cartesian or cocartesian, the

V
-
category
\mathcal{DA}
is precisely the category of lenses or prisms over

\mathcal{A}
, respectively. Moreover, one of the main results of
(
Pastro and Street 2008
, Proposition
6.1)
declares that the
V
-
category
[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{C},{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}]
of copresheaves over these

V
-
categories is equivalent to the
V
-
category of
Tambara modules on
\mathbf{C}
. In the case of lenses or prisms, these Tambara
modules are precisely cartesian and cocartesian profunctors, and this correspondence justifies
their profunctor representation.

We will see how the results of Pastro and Street can be directly applied to the
theory of optics, although they are not general enough for our purposes.
Milewski
Milewski 2017
already proposed a unified description of optics, later
extended by
Boisseau and Gibbons 2018
and
Riley 2018
, that requires a
generalization of the original result by Pastro and Street from monoidal
products to arbitrary monoidal actions. In order to capture
V
-
enriched mixed
optics, we need to go even further and generalize the definition of Tambara
module in two directions. The monoidal category
\mathcal{A}
in their definition
needs to be substituted by a pair of arbitrary categories
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
, and
the monoidal product
\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
needs to be
substituted by a pair of arbitrary monoidal actions

.

This section can be seen both as a partial exposition of one of the main results
of the work of Pastro and Street
(
Pastro and Street 2008
, Proposition 6.1)
and a
generalization of their definitions and propositions to the setting that is most
useful for applications in functional programming.

4.1
Generalized Tambara modules

Originally,
Tambara modules

Tambara 2006
were conceived of as a
structure on top of certain profunctors that made them play nicely with
some monoidal action in both their covariant and contravariant components.

For our purposes, Tambara modules represent the different ways in which we can
use an optic. If a profunctor
P
has Tambara module structure for the monoidal
actions defining an optic, we can use that optic to lift the profunctor applied
to the foci,
P(A,B)
, to the full structures,
P(S,T)
. For instance, the
profunctor
(-)\times B\to(-)
can be used to lift the projection

A\times B\to B
into the
update
function
S\times B\to T
. In other
words, this profunctor is a Tambara module compatible with all the families of
optics that admit an
update
function, such as
lenses
. In
programming libraries, that profunctor can be used to define a polymorphic
update

combinator that works across different families of optics.

Formally, we want to prove that Tambara modules for the actions

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},\otimes,I)
be a monoidal
V
-
category with two monoidal actions

(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\hbox to12.55pt{\vbox to12.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.27544pt\lower-6.27544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

For an arbitrary
V
, Tambara modules are the objects of a
V
-
category

{{\emph{\mathbf{Tamb}}}}
whose hom-object from
(P,\alpha)
to
(Q,\alpha^{\prime})
is
computed as the equalizer of the arrows

\displaystyle\int_{X,Y}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(P(X,Y),Q(X,Y)

\displaystyle)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\phantom{AAAAAAA}\pi_{A,B}\phantom{AAAAAAA}}\

\displaystyle{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(P(A,B),Q(A,B))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\phantom{AAAAA}{\hyperlink{linkvenrichment}{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathrm{id},\alpha^{\prime}_{A,B})\phantom{AAAAA}}\

.

Remark 4.2
.

Pastro and Street
Pastro and Street 2008
follow the convention of omitting the
unitors and the associators of the monoidal category when defining Tambara
modules. These appear in
Definition

4.1
replaced by the
structure isomorphisms of the two monoidal actions.

4.2
Pastro-Street’s “double” comonad

Tambara modules are coalgebras for a particular comonad
(
Pastro and Street 2008
, §5)
in profunctors.
That comonad has a left adjoint that must therefore be a monad, and then Tambara
modules can be equivalently described as algebras for that monad. We will
describe the
V
-
category of
generalized
Tambara modules
Tamb
as an
Eilenberg-Moore category first for a comonad and then for its left adjoint
monad. This will be the main lemma (
Lemma

4.6
) towards the
profunctor representation theorem (
Theorem

4.14
).

Remark 4.3
.

Before the definition, let us recall the axioms for a monoidal
V
-
action

\oslash\colon\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
of a monoidal
V
-
category

(\mathbf{M},\otimes,I)
with coherence isomorphisms
(a,\lambda,\rho)
to an
arbitrary category
\mathbf{C}
. Let

\displaystyle\phi_{A}\colon A\cong I\oslash A,

\displaystyle\phi_{M,N,A}\colon M\oslash(N\oslash A)\cong(M\otimes N)\oslash A,

be the structure
V
-
natural isomorphisms of the monoidal action. Note that the following are precisely the axioms for a strong
monoidal functor
\mathbf{M}\to[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
written as
\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
; they
are simplified by the fact that
[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}]
is strict.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\oslash M\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash I\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(I\otimes M)\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\otimes I)\oslash A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{I,M,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{M\oslash A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash\phi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{M,I,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{M}\oslash A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{M}\oslash A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash N\oslash K\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash N\oslash K\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\otimes N)\oslash K\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash(N\otimes K)\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((M\otimes N)\otimes K)\oslash A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\otimes(N\otimes K))\oslash A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{M,N,K\oslash A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oslash\phi_{N,K,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{M\otimes N,K,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{M,N\otimes K,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{M,N,K}\oslash A}

Proposition 4.5
.

The
V
-
functor
\Theta
can be endowed with a comonad structure. Its
counit is the
V
-
natural family of morphisms

\varepsilon_{P,A,B}\colon\Theta P(A,B)\to P(A,B)
obtained by
projecting on the monoidal unit and applying the unitors,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P(A,B)}

Finally, by the universal property of the end, that implies
that
\Theta\varepsilon_{P}\circ\delta_{P}
must coincide with the identity.

Let us now prove right counitality,

\varepsilon_{\Theta P}\circ\delta_{P}=\mathrm{id}_{\Theta P}
, which follows from
the commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta\Theta P(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P[I](A,B)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[I][M](A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[M](A,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I\otimes M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\Theta P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P(\phi_{I},\phi_{I}^{-1R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\phi_{M,I,A},\phi^{-1R}_{M,I,B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\phi_{A},\phi_{B}^{-1R})}

Again, the definition of
\delta
makes the left pentagon
commute. The upper right square commutes now because of the definition of

\varepsilon
, whereas the lower right square commutes because of
functoriality of ends and naturality of
\pi
. By the axioms of the monoidal
actions (Remark
4.3
), the bottom edge of the diagram
can be rewritten as

P(\phi_{A}^{L},\phi_{B}^{-1R})\circ P(\phi_{M,I,A}^{L},\phi^{-1R}_{M,I,B})=P(\rho_{M}^{-1}\oslash A,\rho_{M}\oslash B).

Now, by the wedge condition of the end, the left-bottom side
of the previous diagram is just the projection
\pi_{M}
.

Finally, by the universal property of the end,

\varepsilon_{\Theta P}\circ\delta_{P}
must coincide with the identity.

Coassociativity,

\Theta\delta_{P}\circ\delta_{P}=\delta_{\Theta P}\circ\delta_{P}
, follows from
commutativity of the following diagram in
Figure

11
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta P(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta\Theta P(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta\Theta P(A,B)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\phi_{M,N\otimes K,A},\phi^{-1R}_{M,N\otimes K,B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(a^{-1}_{M,N,K},a_{M,N,K})}

Figure 11:
Diagram for the coassociativity axiom.

We need to show that the upper diamond commutes; by the universal property of
the ends, this amounts to showing that it commutes when followed by

\pi_{M}\circ\pi_{N}\circ\pi_{K}
. The lower pentagon is made of isomorphisms,
and it commutes by the axioms of the monoidal actions
(
Remark

4.3
). The two upper degenerate pentagons commute by
definition of
\delta
. The two trapezoids commute by functoriality of ends
and naturality of the projections.

Finally, the two outermost morphisms of the diagram correspond to two
projections from
\Theta P(A,B)
, namely
\pi_{(M\otimes N)\otimes K}
and

\pi_{M\otimes(N\otimes K)}
. The wedge condition for the associator

a_{M,N,K}
makes the external diagram commute.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Tambara modules are precisely coalgebras for this comonad. There exists an
isomorphism of
V
-
categories
\hyperlink{linktamb}{\mathbf{Tamb}}\cong EM(\Theta)
from the
category of Tambara modules to the Eilenberg-Moore category of
\Theta
.

Proof.

Note that the object of
V
-
natural transformations from
P
to

\Theta P
is precisely

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prof}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})(P,\Theta P)

\displaystyle\cong

{
Natural transformation
}

whose elements can be seen as a family of morphisms that is dinatural in

M\in\mathbf{M}
and natural in
(A,B)\in\mathbf{C}^{op}\otimes\mathbf{D}
. The two conditions in the
definition of Tambara module can be rewritten as the axioms of the coalgebra.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

The
\Theta
comonad has a left
V
-
adjoint
\Phi
, which must therefore
be a monad. On objects, it is given by the following formula.
